## Wire

Wire was a manufactured length of flexible metal, a "coiled good" alongside chain and rope. It was extremely useful and in wide demand across the Realms for everything from common tools to fancy jewelry to elaborate mechanisms.

Description
Wire was often quite brittle and soft from the drawing process, making it quite easy to snap under the right circumstances. Bending wire more than once in the same spot could snap it, and most wire produced was quite thick to ensure its durability.

Hard metals and alloys could respond poorly to being drawn into wire and become more fragile, while softer metals such as gold were the easiest to draw into fine wire. Adamant in particular was difficult to draw into wire because of its extreme brittleness, and had to be alloyed with molybdenum to make it usable.

Availability

Most blacksmiths could make relatively thick wire as needed, but thinner and more delicate wire was much rarer. Crafted by wiredrawers, the expertise and tools required to make good fine wire made it harder to come by in much of Faerûn compared to rope and chain, with the centers of such production found in the Calimshan and Tashalar regions. In many human communities, urban gnome and dwarf artisans pioneered fine wire and chain crafting, and gnomes in particular led the development of increasingly thin and flexible wire that could be used for everyday purposes.

Trade

 Thanks to the consistent demand, "manygoods" traders tried to keep at least some wire in their inventory, as well as a chisel and two pairs of pliers for cutting off lengths on demand.
 Aurora's Emporium provided common wire by the foot (30 centimeters) in a variety of metals, including brass, bronze, copper, gold, iron, lead, mithral, platinum, silver, steel, and tin. Higher-quality lengths of wire were also available for use in priestly rituals and wizardly studies, at a much higher price.
 Gleskur's Finemetals of Secomber produced fine, braided copper wire.
 The Splendid Order of Armorers, Locksmiths, and Finesmiths and the Jewelers' Guild of Waterdeep made, traded, and used wire for sculpting and ornamentation.
 Mhaer's Alley in Waterdeep had a great concentration of shops that sold tailoring supplies, including fine wire.
 Rallogar Hardware in Wheloon sold all sorts of tools and supplies, including great stocks of rope, cable, and wire.
 Nelzol's Notions in Immersea sold various hardware supplies, including coils of fine wire.
 The Chandlers Guild of Ravens Bluff oversaw maritime tools and equipment, including the development of new kinds of wired cable.
 The city of Khôltar was devoted to metalworking, and the shop of Oraundro Nempeth's sold rope, cord, twine, wire, tying rings, and chain.

Usages

 Wire was coiled on a lathe, then cut to make rings, such as for making chainmail armor. The process of coiling, cutting, knitting, and riveting them was extremely tedious and delicate. Copperbright armor resembled scale armor, and was held together with extensive wiring.
 Cable was an unusual form of woven rope that had a twisted wire core to give it incredible strength.
 As a strong, stiff, malleable line, wire was popular with merchants for tying down loose cargo.
 Fashionable clothing, elaborate hairstyles and costumes, and accessories such as bustles and stomachers used wire to shape and support their forms, and many forms of jewelry used wire in their construction. The finest, most flexible wire (such as gold) could be used similarly to thread in clothing. Wire was not, however, used in making shaped brassieres or corsets.
 Wire was a common form of ornamentation for all sorts of objects.
 Wire loops were used to store holed coins such as the authokh.
 Stringed instruments could use metal wire for their strings.
 Wired canes used a hard-to-see wire support to facilitate thefts.
 Some garottes used a length of flexible, strong wire between two handles to throttle victims.
 Traps could use wire for a strong, hard-to-see trigger mechanism. Specially sharpened wire could be used as a trap itself, especially when stretched at neck-height.
 Clockwork devices used wire as part of their mechanisms.
 Professional lockpicks used lengths of wire to manipulate locks, and any short piece of sturdy wire could be used as an improvised lockpick.
 Basic hinging mechanisms could be made from a simple loop of wire, such as in sparkers.
 Taxidermized animals were often held together with metal wire, and entire skeletons could be wired together to be kept intact.
 Fine woven wire meshes could be used to stop small blades, and found some use in making thief-resistant coin pouches, as well as masks for practicing fencing. Larger wire meshes could be used to make fences for animals.
 Combs and brushes often used fine metal wire for their teeth and bristles.
 Small cages for animals and insects could be made from wire.
 Spectacles were made of wire wrapped around the glass lens.
 Priests of Gond were fond of gathering up small, useful bits of junk such as wire.
 Loviatans celebrated the Rite of Pain and Purity by dancing on sharp objects, including barbed wire.

Notable Instances

 The Iron Bow of Gesen had a golden wire bowstring.
 The Machine of Lum the Mad contained a vast profusion of wires.
 The Sash of Sune was stitched in thread-of-gold.
 A trap-springer was made from a spiral of flattened wire.

Spell Components
A great number of spells used various forms of wire as material components, especially those involving communication and thought.
 Alarm used a small bell and a piece of fine silver wire.
 Alter beast used a pair of golden wires twisted into a double helix.
 Arcane mark ward used a small bell and a piece of fine silver wire.
 Demand used a piece of copper wire.
 Descent into madness used a complicated gold wire cube representing a tesseract.
 Detect spirits used a copper wire pendant.
 Gedlee's electric loop used some copper wire.
 Grounding used a small coil of silver wire.
 Harp of war used a thin piece of silver wire.
 Hovering road used a loop of gold wire.
 Juggernaut could use a bit of wire.
 Khelben's warding whip used a piece of wire and a pinch of powdered electrum.
 Levitate could use a piece of golden wire bent into the shape of a cup.
 Lightning rod used a piece of copper wire.
 Message used a short piece of copper wire.
 Obold's brightness used a tangle of wire.
 Protection from electricity used a piece of copper wire.
 Protection from electricity, 10' radius used one or more pieces of copper piece.
 Rebinding used a skein of silver wire.
 Restore rune could use wire among its reagents.
 Rising colossus used a bit of wire, thread or fiber.
 Sacred link could use a strand of fine wire.
 Sending used a piece of fine copper wire.
 The arcane version of shape metal required a wire to be bent into an approximation of the shape that a caster wished to affect upon their target.
 Suspension used a leather loop suspended from a bent golden wire.
 Thunderhead used a small piece of copper wire.
 Virus charm used a valuable web of thin, finely-spun copper wire.
 Whirlwave'' used a piece of wire or glue-stiffened thread.

History
Thread-of-gold was an exceptionally fine wire made by ancient dwarves, though the process of crafting it was long lost to modern dwarves.

Wire was made by the ancient human peoples of Faerûn, including the Rengarth of Netheril thousands of years before, and by the people of Calimshan by -6048 DR.

The consistent production of fine wire in Faerûn was still a developing art by the mid–14 century DR, as artisans continued to gradually refine the process over time. It was expected to still be an area of experimentation and notable improvements through to the mid–16 century DR.

Cable—rope woven around wire—was first developed around the mid-14 century DR.

## Winged horror

Winged horrors were a variety of flying ghouls that were encountered throughout the Shadow-cursed Lands in the Western Heartlands during the 15 century DR.

Description
While they resembled ghouls in appearance and structure, winged horrors were smaller and weighed less than their land-bound cousins.

Behavior
These creatures were commanded by Absolutists to swoop down and seize important assets or kidnap individuals.

Abilities
They could fly at a rate of  per minute.

Combat
Like other ghouls, winged horrors could paralyze foes with their bite. Certain races such as elves and other undead were immune to this effect.

History
In the, the ex-Flaming Fist soldier Marcus led several winged horrors in his planned abduction of Isobel, for General Ketheric Thorm.

Notable Locales
A pair of winged horrors preferred to perch atop the battlements of Moonrise Towers.

## Wizardsbane oil

Wizardsbane oil was a type of magic oil used to temporarily handicap wizards, sorcerers, and other spellcasters.

Description
The oil was an obviously magical substance. When one peered into the vial, they could see symbols for the eight schools of magic shift and move in the oil, appearing and dissolving one at a time.

Powers
The oil could be applied to weapons or thrown at the targets, shattering the vials. Those spellcasters who were touched by the oil found it difficult to concentrate on magic, began to misfire or miscast spells, and successfully cast magics were more likely to break and disperse.

Creation
Wizardsbane oil was created by alchemically extracting the essence from gremishka tails and mining it with certain types of alchemical suspension.

## Wizard's Torc

The Wizard's Torc was a magic item that opened a secret passage from the Circle of Ualair in the Speculum Grounds in Myth Drannor to the Elven Catacombs under Castle Cormanthor.

Description
The Wizard's Torc was a piece of jewelry that consisted of two parts. The first was a large neck ring. The ring was decorated with a starstone.

Powers
The Wizard's Torc granted the wielder the power to teleport from the Circle of Ualair into a circular room at the beginning of the Elven Catacombs. The bearer of the Wizard's Torc was directly transported to the Catacombs' First Cellar.

History
It is unknown what happened to the Wizard's Torc after Myth Drannor was destroyed at the end of the Weeping War. Some claim that Ualair broke apart the Wizard's Torc separating the frame and the starstone to seal the passage. Either way, part of the magical item came into the possession of Preybelish, a dark naga, who took up residence behind a magical door in the Dwarven Dungeons under the ruins of the elven city.

In the, a group of adventurers captured the broken torc from Preybelish and got a starstone to complete the Wizard's Torc from Harldain Ironbar, a city engineer of Myth Drannor and the leader of the dwarves of Onaglym, the House of Gems.

## Wine box of holding

Wine boxes of holding were a variety of bags of holding created and enchanted to be used for wine bottles exclusively.

Description
Wine boxes of holding were beautifully decorated oaken containers. Their wooden surfaces were a work of art, being lovingly carved with images of plant life, leaves, and grapevines.

Powers
When opened, a wine box of holding appeared to only have space to hold a single bottle of wine; however, upon closer examination, it was obvious that its enchantments allowed for six bottles. Only one bottle was visible in the box, and once it was removed, the next stored bottle took its place.

History
Circa, a precious wine box of holding belonged to the maedar winemaker Vorlon, who ran the Morden Winery located in a valley outside the village of Dragon Falls in the Vast. His extradimensional box contained more treasures: an enchanted bottle of the healer and a rare crystal bottle of elverquisst bottled the ancient Myth Drannor. The maedar offered the box and its contents to the adventurers who helped him work out a deal between his winery and its rightful owner, Shauna Morden of Ravens Bluff.

## Witch of the Dragon Waves

The Witch of the Dragon Waves was a leaky cog.

Harnrim Starangh boarded the Witch of the Dragon Waves as a stowaway while it was docked in Marsember in Cormyr, after his scheme to steal the latest spell devised by Vangerdahast was dashed and thwarted by Elminster Aumar in the . He used magic to turn himself into one of the ship's ballast stones. At that time, the ship was loaded with pickled beef.

Separately, the ship was boarded by Aumun Tholant Bezrar and Malakar Surth, as passengers rather than stowaways, seeking to go to Yhaunn in Sembia while trying to escape Marsember, as they too had been involved in Starangh's latest scheme. While the two had a disagreement shortly after raising anchors, it was interrupted by the appearance of a dragon matching the description of Aeglyl Dreadclaw.

## Will-o'-wisp language

The Will-o'-wisp language was an entirely optical language used by will-o'-wisps, Nyths, and variants found in the Domains of Dread, such as will o'deeps and will o'seas.

Description
Described by some as merely a "flickering light" language, will-o'-wisps and its other "speakers" conveyed ideas through changing the color and or the brightness or intensity of their light-based bodies.

The alien and subtle nature of this language made it largely incomprehensible to the average human, demihuman, and most other creatures. It was considered by many to be impossible to mimic, though some swamp-based druids managed to somewhat communicate in the language through the use of light sources, such as a hooded lantern.

## Wink and Kiss (Arabel)

The Wink and Kiss was a decent tavern in Arabel.

Location
The tavern was found on the outermost street of Arabel, just inside of the city walls. It was a short distance east of the southern Calantar's Gate and Calantar's Way, which led to Immersea and Suzail, and a block west down the same street from the Nine Fires inn.

Services
The Wink and Kiss was known around Arabel as a place to get good meals and gossip.

History
During Gondegal's war in 1352 DR, the Cormyrean counter-attack was to be conducted by magically blasting the wall apart. Baron Thomdor Ammaeth sadly noted that the Wink and Kiss would likely be destroyed in the process, but before the assault could commence Gondegal vanished and his army collapsed.

The tavern suffered a mysterious fire in 1365 DR during the Night of Masks festival, burning at least partially down.

The Puppetmasters gang of adventurers used the Wink and Kiss as their home base while working on schemes in 1369 DR.

## Will o'sea

A Will o'sea was an oceanic creatures loosely related to will-o'-wisps, unique to the Domains of Dread.

Description
These creatures had insubstantial bodies, appearing as shifting cascades of glowing energy, in luxuriant shades and colors. On average they measured . They were at times almost indistinguishable from what people on some worlds would refer to as St. Elmo's fire. They were quite agile fliers, capable of suddenly switching between placid hovering and frantic jerky flight patterns.

Personality
A will o'sea was a highly intelligent creature and far more aggressive than its more common land-based counterparts.

Abilities
These creatures notably lacked a will-o'-wisp's ability to turn invisible, but possessed some level of shapeshifting ability, which they often used to take the form of a ship or lighthouse for the purposes of drawing in prey. Additionally, they were capable of shooting out bolts of electricity.

They could be harmed physically by weaponry, they were impervious to harm from most magic. The only forms of magic that appeared to harm them were cold-based, such as the spells cone of cold and ice storm.

Combat
The will o'sea preferred to hunt through deception and trickery, rather than direct attacks, shapeshifting itself over top of sharp rocks, underwater reefs, or other hazards in a ploy to draw prey towards it. Their most common ploys were to take the form of a burning ship, a tactic that particularly worked well for them during storms, or using their shimmering beneath the water to trick sailors into believing that there was sunken treasure below.

They would typically go out if their way to directly kill as few people as possible in the shipwrecks they caused, preferring to allow their victims to drown so that they may feast off their mental energies. They would often hover sailors, attempting to thwart any attempts they made to return to land. If any victims of shipwreck were escaping, a will o'sea would fire at them with its electrical attack, though would never unleash this whilst underwater.

Society
The will o'sea was a solitary nocturnal creature, typically coming out during the dusk or evening.
Diet
These creatures fed off of the electrical energy generated by the brains of humans and demihumans, particularly the emotional energy given off from those panicking from drowning.
Homelands
Within the Domains of Dread the will o'sea made their homes along coast lines, typically near shipwrecks, and were commonly encountered just off of common sea routes. They could be in most climates, most commonly tropical areas, though were never found in arctic areas. One Domain of Dread they were particularly found in was the Sea of Sorrows. If a hunting area ever ceased to be viable, they would simply move on and find somewhere else to haunt for prey.
Languages
These creatures communicated in the manner common amongst will-o'-wisps, utilizing a variety of electrical snaps, color shifts, clicks and hums.
Relationships
The hunting grounds and lairs of will o'sea were commonly host to ghosts, sea zombies, and even entire ghost ships. These undead rarely interfered with will o'sea, though at times could keep sailors from entering their area.

Rumors & Legends
Some seafarers in the Domains of Dread spoke rumors of there being undersea creatures that could communicate with them.

History
Around the, as the Grand Conjunction was nearing in the Domains of Dread the boundaries between that plane of existence and the Prime Material plane were weakening, allowing for portals to open sporadically between the two around the Vast.  From one of these portals came a will o'sea, that appeared in Ravens Bluff around Tym's Supple Leather Shoppe and attacked a group of adventurers. According to some accounts, these same adventurers might have fought more later that day, having been summoned by a sea spawn master.

## Wine Blight Insurrection

The Wine Blight Insurrection was a conflict between wine-producing peasant farmers in the Purple Hills and their lord, the Duke of Ankramir, in the summer of the .

History
Causes
A vine-blight struck Faerûn in 1252 DR, causing grapes to die and resulting in a shortage of wine. This created hardship for the wine-producing peasants of the Purple Hills in Tethyr, which was exacerbated when the Duke of Ankramir made it known that he expected his usual levy of wine from them. Instead, the destitute peasants ignored him sought to recoup their losses by selling their wine stores to buyers in the North. Incensed, the Duke sent his soldiers to confiscate the wine.

Battle
The only notable battle of the Wine Blight Insurrection was the so-called Battle of the Broken Vats, in which the peasants defiantly smashed many of their casks of wine and threw them into the Sea of Swords to prevent the Duke's soldiers from seizing them. In retaliation, the Duke ordered his troops to capture the head of every household in the Purple Hills.

Aftermath
The captured peasants were to be hastily tried for treason by a council of judges and then put to death. However, a priest of Ilmater named Ramedar came to their aid, and eloquently argued that the rebels should be released into the custody of the church of Ilmater. His pleas successfully saved hundreds of lives and cemented his reputation as a saint of his faith, especially once the enraged Duke had him put to death for treason soon after.

## Wilaver Chandler

History

During the Sschindylryn invasion of Eveningstar, the drow forces of Vicala Szind attacked the farmhouse and captured the entire family alive so that she could interrogate them about the Thread of the Weave. Unknown to the drow, the houseservant Maisby had escaped back to Eveningstar and sought help, getting first a pair of Purple Dragons and then a team of adventurers to investigate the farmhouse. Unfortunately, Vicala Szind had set all ten Midlers and Chandlers as part of a sacrificial life-draining spell, and the rescuers would have had to stealthily rescue each family member without Vicala noticing, lest she spitefully kill them.

Wilaver was kept in the cellar with his wife Caryn and grandson Thoben, guarded by drow warriors until their fate was decided.

## Winterflower Duskmere

Lady Winterflower Duskmere was an elven magician during the Time of Dragons, and a heir to House Duskmere. She was directly responsible for the Dracorage mythal.

Description
Her exquisiite features added up to a look of courage and intelligence. She had curly black hair, gold-flecked blue eyes, and fair skin.

Personality
Winterflower behaved in extremes, to other people, showing herself as either cold and aloof or warm and friendly. However, this was deliberate on her part, to better draw the attentions of the men around her and thus manipulate them to her ends. Her ultimate end, as she explained, was advancing elven freedom, bringing them away from the control of the wyrms.

Abilities
Lady Winterflower's singing was impressive and touching.

She was a mage of some power, being able to cast dimensional anchor. Her skill was sufficient that she quickly mastered the workings of Rhespen's own staff, casting spells from it from the moment she touched it, and she was almost certainly involved in the creation of the Dracorage mythal.

Possessions
Lady Winterflower Duskmere possessed a number of spellbooks, which she was not allowed to bring to Dawnfire. She also received a number of gifts from King Orchtrien, notably, a bracelet in the shape of a spiraling dragon. Her room in the palace was initially furnished exclusively with elven finery. The lady's most prized possession was a jewelry box.

Relationships
She tried to seduce King Orchtrien, and became his mistress for a time, but her true love was for Rhespen Ash.

History
-25,090 DR
After the houses Duskmere, Vilirith and Starfall failed in an attempt to ambush Orchtrien's forces, the King compelled the heads to send forth their heirs to his abode, so that they may be educated, wooed, and held hostage, thus ensuring their houses' ongoing good behavior. Of the three, Winterflower Duskmere came to Dawnfire in a closed coach; when Rhespen Ash, the king's deputy who was supposed to introduce them, noticed that, he cast a spell of invisibility on the coach, thwarting the Lady's desire not to look at the city. She agreed to ride up the rest of the road, and talked with Rhespen Ash along the way; she was extremely skeptical of the goodness of King Orchtrien, and of his goals.

Despite her aloofness, her singing in the Elven Quarter of Dawnfire became quite popular, and finally, at one feast, the King called on her to sing a song. She chose one about a king whose misrule and insanity brought calamity and misfortune, making the king's deputy, Maldur Breakstone, to chastise her with magic for the impertinence. Rhespen Ash took her side, claiming she had meant no offense, and the King bid his two deputies duel for her honor.

After the duel, Lady Winterflower opened up to Rhespen, admitted she'd meant offense, and thanked him for helping her. She was later called on to sing at another feast; this one proceeded without issue, though the king took an interest in her afterwards. Rhespen Ash later visited Winterflower and told her that Orchtrien, later having learned of his feelings towards her, sent him in a dangerous winter raiding commission that took him from Dawnfire for the whole season. She was aghast.

-25,089 DR
Despite her initial outrage, Winterflower became the King's mistress over winter. Per Orchtrien, she warmed up to him and claimed to fall in love with the king over time. By the return of Rhespen Ash in early spring, Winterflower showed no outward signs of love for him during his hero's welcome, but while walking with Orchtrien back to his chambers, she gave Rhespen signs of distress.

When Rhespen turned up at her door, she claimed to be afflicted by an intermittent enchantment that made him fall heads over heels for the king, granting her precious few reprieves from the magic. In response, Rhespen erased the encounter from her mind with a spell, and set forth. He returned after copying the King's spells, and used one spell to erase the enchantment from her mind; she acted as though released, but their door was then kicked in by Maldur and several of Orchtrien's crossbowmen. Winterflower quickly seized the copied spellbooks, Rhespen's staff, and teleported away.

At the hideout of the Duskmere, Winterflower and other mages took efforts to decipher, first, the spells of concealment against magical search, and that was all they'd managed to achieve before Rhespen Ash snuck inside and tried to retrieve the books. He disabled the other mages, but Winterflower stopped him with a dimensional anchor spell; while they talked, Orchtrien found the stronghold. Rhespen's plan to flee with the books and present them to his King thwarted, he agreed to join Winterflower's rebellion, and slow Orchtrien down in combat so that at least some of the books could be taken to other elves.

Lady Duskmere Winterflower was the last and only person who managed to escape with one of Orchtrien's books after the king's men razed the hidden stronghold. She was tempted to discard the book, but decided to persevere in order to make the sacrifices worth it. That eventually led to the creation of the Dracorage mythal.

Rumors & Speculation
During a discussion between Rhespen Ash, tortured after turning traitor, and King Orchtrien, the gold dragon surmised that she'd convinced him of her mind control to prompt him towards assassination, and that she'd seduced him too, in order to better spy and perhaps commit regicide.

## Wizard gong

Wizard gong was a rare arcane spell from the alteration and evocation schools of magic that was used as a silent alarm by wizards on the world of Oerth.

Effects
The spell's target was a door, window, portal, lid, or similar egress that was touched by the spellcaster in the process. Once wizard gong was cast, it remained active indefinitely until the alarm went off or until the caster left the same plane of existence as the ensorcelled object. Whilst the wizard gong remained active, the wizard could not access the portion of their memory where the spell was stored, even with rest, study, or through magical recovery. Only one wizard gong could be active at a time. When active, the wizard gong spell was undetectable and unremovable, could not be disrupted by any means, and radiated no magic.

Once the ensorcelled egress was opened, breached, broken, or destroyed, the wizard received the feeling of silent alarm in the form of vibration.

Components
Wizard gong required verbal, somatic, and material components to be cast. The material component for the spell was a clear and colorless gemstone worth not less than 1,000 gp. The gem was not consumed in the process.

History
The wizard gong spell was known to Rautheene of Greyhawk, and it was among the spells exchanged between the members of the Wizards Three during their visit to Earth and raiding the larders of Ed Greenwood of Canada. The spell was brought over to the world of Toril by Elminster of Shadowdale, a member of the same group.

## Wilder

Wilders were a tradition of psionics users that drew upon raw emotion and passion to fuel and trigger their abilities. Within the scope of psionic traditions, wilders stood as opposites to the disciplined and meditative psions, though the two drew from the same well of psionic powers.

Culture
Wilders and psions were among the most commonly found form of psionics users in the Realms. Among human regions, they most frequently arose in areas that were built overtop of the ruins of ancient psionic civilizations. This included the Vilhon Reach, where the psiocracy of Jhaamdath once stood, and anywhere that stood above duergar or illithid cities. Wilders could also frequently be found in areas with strong wild psionic fluctuations. Other regions where wilders could commonly be found included the Shaar.

In contrast to psions, wielders of the Invisible Art that largely required some form of schooling to further unlock their psionic powers, the abilities of a wilder were always innate and required no schooling, making most largely self-taught. However, not all eschewed academics, for in the mid-1370s DR at least two of the instructors at the College of the Eclipse in Riatavin were wilders, and many wilders received training in Skullport's Hall of Mental Splendor to become cognition thieves.

Often times, wilders arose from those who were left on their own for extended periods during childhood, sometimes forced by outside circumstances to live in the wilderness. When returned to civilization, such wilders would often strive to impress others with their psionics in an effort to ameliorate the many years they had spent in solitude.

Their desire to use the Invisible Art was often paramount, though a wilder would not turn down currency or items that could potentially enhance their abilities.

Wilders were more likely to spring up among humans than any other race. Elves, gnomes, and halflings were usually incapable of putting themselves in the emotional moment required for wilders to trigger psionic abilities, though those among the ghostwise halflings who displayed psionic powers had no such difficulties and often chose to follow this tradition. Some rebellious dwarves found the way of the wilder appealing. And the tradition was overall similar to the culture of half-giants. When raised in communities where their kind was sparse, blue goblins were liable to take up the path of a wilder. And some aboleths developed themselves as wilders.
Religion
Wilders were often too focused on themselves to devote themselves to the philosophy of a deity or organized religion.
Relationships
For most of the general populace in the Realms, psionics and wilders alike were unrecognized or barely understood, often confusing them with traditional spellcasters of magic. Consequently, a commoner's view of a wilder could differ little from their attitude and reactions towards arcane spellcasters, which in many cases could mean a sense of distrust

Among the other traditions of psionics, psions were often distrustful of them due to their philosophical differences and soulknives were somewhat uncomfortable around them, but wilders themselves were amicable towards interacting with all sorts of users of the Invisible Art.

Personality
This tradition of psionics users were found to run the whole gamut of alignments. They were typically rather adventurous, turbulent, somewhat impatient, and above all eager to utilize their psionic powers. They delighted in interacting with others and the undertaking dangerous quests, as both were ideal opportunities to use and showcase their powers.

Abilities
Wilders manifested their powers through the sheer force and passion of their emotions, the abilities growing more powerful the greater a wilder's passion towards whatever they were doing. Unlike wizards, who had to prepare their spells, a wilder's psionic powers were ingrained within their mind and thus always at the ready.

Early into their psionic development, wilders acquired such acute intuition that they became deftly aware of touch-based attacks and were capable of avoiding them with ease.

Due to their temperamental personalities, wilders struggled with the use of powers from the telepathy discipline. This difficulty with using telepathic powers became amplified for a wilder as their mastery of psionic powers improved. However, those who undertook training to become cognition thieves overcame this mental block.
Wild Surge
Through the fervor in which they pursued the use of their powers, wilders developed a unique ability to wild surge. This was a literal surging manifestation of their psionic powers, far beyond what they were normally capable of, making their emotions rise to the surface. A wilder could trigger this whenever they manifested a power, however, every use of wild surge had potential to psychically strain the wilder, what was known as a psychic enervation. Thus, wilders stood at risk of injuring themselves through careless use of the power.

In time, wilders developed a sense of euphoria while wild surging that could improve their general capabilities within combat.

Notable Wilders
 Dalthoeneir Delthoenoth, a moon elf wilder and member of the Kaliesh'erai.
 Jacenelle Traen, a Chondathan wilder who led the adventuring company Stray Thoughts in the 1370s DR.

## Windpennant Pillars

Windpennant Pillars was a townhouse in the North Ward of Waterdeep in the 14 century DR.

Location
The townhouse was in the middle of a row of business shops. There was a large, grand mansion behind it.

Structure
The building sprawled over the other businesses. Inside, there were numerous offices, and a stable that connected to a manor behind it through a large iron door.

Activities
The place was for rent.

Defenses
The knocker on the door was trapped, making a planter fall on top of anyone who used it.

History
Laeral Silverhand, in her guise as Trennan Beldrusk, investigated Auvrarn Labraster starting by his rented lodgings at some point in ; she uncovered barely any signs of habitation. She fell into a trap on a magically darkened room on the stable, landing herself in an underground passage, which ultimately led to the mansion behind it.

## William Windsphere

William Windsphere was the bartender of the Old Man's Face in the Cormyrean village of Tyrluk in the late 15 century DR.

History

In the mid-1480s DR, William had given a spare key to his tavern to the innkeeper of the Old Man's Face, Branson Blackboot, in case he locked himself outside the tavern. William's tavern was visited by the Cormyrean adventurer-noble Oriphaun Huntsilver, who hid a fragment of the Abyssal Planus Mechanus Nether Scroll at the Old Man's Face by scribbling a promise of payment for services rendered on the blank side of the scroll, which William pinned up in the taproom amid other receipts and notes.

Soon after, an army of sellswords led by the Shadovar wizard Tarys came down the High Road and broke into the inn in search of it. While William hid himself in a crate in the tavern's basement, Branson locked himself in his office, where he stayed until rescued by Harper adventurers also searching for the scroll fragment. Branson gave the adventurers his key to the barn, where he kept the tavern key that they then used to rescue William (who was also stuck inside his crate). After defeating Tarys the confused William led them to where the Nether Scroll had been put in plain sight, where everyone had walked past it.

## Willac Tatthryd

Willac Tatthryd was an adventuring human priest from Hillsfar whose death in the mid-to-late 14 century DR, led to a reincarnation as an ogre woman.

Description
At the age of twenty-three winters, Willac in her ogre's form was tall, reaching, weighting . She had black hair that the ogress wore in three long braids, dark brown skin, and eyes of the same shade of brown.

Personality
She was a friendly and affectionate individual despite her terrifying, brutish appearance of an ogre. By nature, she did not like violence and often was a voice of reason and kindness when adventuring in a group, even healing her enemies when the battle was over. Willac was quite fond of her armor, polishing the plate mail and shield almost daily.

Abilities
As both a fighter and a priest, Willac was trained in using footman's flail's, footman's maces, horseman's flails, and morning stars. She was also knowledgeable in the topics of healing and religion and was good at horseback-riding and animal handling. As a cleric, she had the following prayers ready on a daily basis: cure light wounds, command, light, detect magic, and hold person. However, in her ogreish body, Willac lost the ability to turn undead. Subsequently, her husband discovered that the true reason for losing that ability was a curse that the two sought adventurers' help in lifting.

Possessions
Her typical equipment included a silver holy symbol and a potion of mammal control. She wore a set of enchanted plate mail of etherealness and carried a small silver shield. As for weapons, Willac carried a footman's mace and a horseman's flail.

Relationships
Willac was truly in love with her human husband, and she appreciated his intelligence, seeking his advice and counsel on all topics.

History
Willac Tatthryd was born a human boy to a poor single farmer father in the city of Hillsfar, in the Moonsea region of Faerûn. Early in life, Willac found religion and realized disdain for a commoner's existence, becoming an adventuring priest when coming of age. At the age of twenty-one, Willac was mauled to death by a bear on the way to the kingdom of Cormyr. The dead priest's temple returned the adventurer to live via the reincarnation spell – the result was unexpected. Willac Tatthryd returned as an ogress. Learning to use the natural benefits of her new body, Willac learned the crude art of being a fighter, and subsequently, she got used to the bulky size, monstrous strength, and her female body, sex, and gender. However, her human peers found being around an ogre to be unnerving, and Willac returned to regular traveling and adventuring when she felt out of place at the temple.

Willac found many adventuring troupes to join, offering her muscles and healing magic. One notable adventure was when her band came across a gang of ogres. The entire group of adventurers was captured, including Willac, and was taken to the ogres' lair to be the main course for dinner. When the ogres' chieftain laid an eye on Willac, he immediately fell in love with the brutish beauty. The ogress managed to get the chieftain drunk by using her feminine woes, and the group fled captivity. Willac got away with the chieftain's plate mail of etherealness.

By the age of twenty-three, she married a fellow adventurer – the human mage Bregubrod Dryctcyni. The wizard courted the ogress for several months until proclaiming his undying love. Willac was not used to a man's affections and this much attention. But despite it all, she developed feelings for the little man, and the two tied the knot.

## Winterglen Claw

The Winterglen Claw, sometimes called the Claw of Winterglen, was a group of human rebels and runaway slaves opposed to the ruling fey of Sarifal in the Moonshae Isles as of the late 15 century DR. It was unclear whether the group referred to themselves as the "Winterglen Claw" or if the name had been bestowed on them by others.

Activities
The Claw's main aspiration was to free the humans of Gwynneth, where the fey of Sarifal had taken over and enslaved the humans who had called it home. They had the boats and resources to travel to other islands as well if needed.

They were a secretive and shadowy faction, but were nevertheless the subject of rumors among the human realms of the Moonshae Isles: some envisioned them as mighty assassins and warriors, while others doubted their existence entirely. Within Sarifal, they were not taken seriously by the proud and immensely powerful ruling leShay, and Sarifal's queen, the High Lady Ordalf, was known to capture and utterly subjugate members of the Claw to serve as her slaves.

Their leader was Captain Rurik, who was himself at least as famous among the humans of the Moonsahes as the Claw itself.

Tactics
The Claw rode on great horses, such as the mighty Cambro draft horse. They had experience with hit-and-run attacks, and a favored tactic was to hide within a forest near Caer Corwell said to have been made dangerous by the Spellplague a century prior (which their fey and elven foes hesitated to enter). They traveled in small groups, and when they camped, they both set sentinels and avoided stripping the saddles and bridles from their steeds.

Relationships
The Claw worked alongside Lord Mindarion of the Llewyrr, who was one of many fey inhabitants of Sarifal who had begun to find leShay rule to be intolerable. Through him, they were loosely aligned with the Knights of Synnoria, however they were unable to work well with other leaders of the Knights, such as Lord Askepel, who refused to acknowledge the humans as a necessary or equal ally.

They had common aims with an alliance of fomorians led by Ughoth, who hated the leShay for having slighted and betrayed them in the past when they imprisoned a fomorian princess, Marabaldia.

When the Winterglen Claw came to the attention of the court of High King Derid Kendrick of Callidyrr, the king took a nominal interest in sponsoring the organization in exchange for their loyalty to him. While this was welcomed by the Claw, many members did not trust the king and considered him a coward.

History

In the late 1470s DR, one of High King Derid's cousins, Lord Aldon Kendrick, made plans to travel to Sarifal aboard the Sphinx to offer vague promises of money and weapons to the rebels in exchange for an oath of loyalty to the king. In truth, his trip was coordinated by Aldon's rivals within the Kendrick family, who contacted High Lady Ordalf about his trip. When the Sphinx arrived at the ruins of Caer Corwell—where Lord Aldon had been told to expect to meet the Claw—they were met by the High Lady herself, pretending to be an agent of Captain Rurik. She personally killed Lord Aldon, and insisted to the crew of the Sphinx that the Winterglen Claw did not exist.

A tenday later, members of the Claw partnered with Mindarion to ride to Caer Corwell to rescue a lycanthrope named Poke from the leShay, because she supposedly knew the location of a missing leShay princess, Amaranth, whom Mindarion and his allies wished to elevate to the throne of Sarifal by overthrowing High Lady Ordalf. In addition to Poke, they rescued Suka, a member of the crew of the Sphinx, and Marabaldia, a long imprisoned fomorian princess.

After losing many men in an ambush by drow soldiers of the leShay, the Claw made their way to the stronghold of Harrowfast to meet with forces of the Llewyrr and of the fomorians in hopes of forging an alliance to overthrow the leShay. Despite having over ten thousand combined troops—sufficient strength to capture Karador, by Captain Rurik's estimate—the negotiations broke down. First, the elves insisted on installing Amaranth on the throne, whereas the Claw wished for no more leShay rulers. Second, the arrogant elves refused to agree or acquiesce to any requests made by the humans or fomorians, including the Claw's demands for the freedom of all human slaves. Finally, an accidental confrontation saw Captain Rurik and Suka mistaken for escaped slaves, leading to them killing three Llewyrr guards, including the Marchlord Oemeril Talos–claere, and forcing the Claw to flee from Harrowfast before the Llewyrr could retaliate. Fighting then broke out between the elves and the fomorians as well. The collapse of the alliance was a major disappointment for the Claw.

Members
The Claw's members were human rebels and former slaves of the fey. It included both Ffolk and Northlanders, who set aside their ancient grudges in the face of the fey threat. They naturally despised the fey of Sarifal, not only for enslaving them, but for the perceived arrogance and sloth that arose from their long lifespans.

## Wind Cavern

Wind Cavern was a cavern in the Horn Hills during the 3 century DR.

Description
The cave was accessible from atop a series of high ravines. It had a narrow opening, and uneven floor throughout; winds made a strange moaning sound passing through the cave.

Geography
Wind Cavern lay within the Horn Hills.

Flora & Fauna
The inside of the cavern had glowing fungi.

Defenses
The entrance was barely accessible. Beyond the cavern was an old dwarven redoubt also known as Lawless Castle by the Knights of Athalantar.

History
During the early 3 century DR, the outlawe Knights of Athalantar used Wind Cavern and Lawless Castle as a base from which they conducted small raids on the forces of Athalantar during the reign of Belaur Aumar. They were found once before, by a wizard they slew; on that year, the cavern and Lawless Castle were found by armsmen of Athalantar, ultimately forcing them to abandon the hideout.

## Witch (fish)

Witch was a species of cold-water flounder that inhabited the eastern currents of the Sea of Moving Ice in Icewind Dale.

Description
A popular northern fairytale stated that a witch coven used these flunders in some dark demonic ritual. These fish were said to have been bled by the witches. In reality, some fishers adamantly insisted that these founders were harmless and carried no foul magics in their blood. It was likely that the witches used them simply because the flounders were easily available, and not because of the fish's demonic magic affinity.

## Wilkan Drome

Wilkan Drome was a servant of Amaunator in the Cormyrean town of Eveningstar in the late 15 century DR.

Description
Wilkan had light skin, blond hair, and wore a brown robe.

History
After the House of the Morning was destroyed in the Sschindylryn invasion of Eveningstar, Wilkan joined Azerie Germond at the ruins of the temple. While Azerie exhorted the faithful, Wilkan acted as a quartermaster of sorts, supplying them with potions of shield of faith and death ward. He also offered up rarer relics to the worthy, including the star of day, breastplate of the shining sun, signet of the shining sun, and the symbol of the shining sun.

## Wizard of Halruaa

Wizards of Halruaa, while looking foppish to many, were among the most powerful spellcasters on Toril. Their special flavor of practicing arcane magic could also be learned by humans outside Halruaa.

Activities
Halruaan wizards were known to engage in trading magical items, and their knowledge in matters arcane connected to these items was nearly unrivaled when compared to other merchants.

Culture
The wizards of Halruaa are noted for their often flashy dress and richly ornamented costumes.

Halruaan wizards often believed that they could use magic to resolve any issue. They were condescending to warriors and knights, no matter how noble, seeing them as no different from barbarians and rogues. In contrast, priests, who practiced divine magic, were considered siblings in magic.

Abilities
Halruaan wizards were well-versed in spellcraft and ancient history.

The magic of these wizards was potent, so much so that it was much harder to escape their spell effects when compared to other arcane casters. They in turn had an easier time escaping from the effects of hostile spells.

Compared to the average wizard or sorcerer, however, they had a tendency to have even less physical gifts.
